in part by a Leinweber Center for Theoretical Physics Graduate Fellowship.
PD, RR and FT are supported by the US Department of Energy under Grant No. DE-SC0013699.

Appendix A
Embedding space formalism

The
\textrm{AdS}_{d+1}
background can be realized as a space-like hypersurface in the
(d{+}2)
dimensional flat spacetime
\mathbb{R}^{d+1,1}
.
Under Cartesian coordinates
X^{A}=(X^{\mathsf{a}},X^{d},X^{d+1})
, the hypersurface is given by the equation

\displaystyle(X^{0})^{2}+(X^{1})^{2}+\cdots+(X^{d-1})^{2}+(X^{d})^{2}-(X^{d+1})^{2}=-R^{2}\,.

(A.1)

The Poincaré coordinates of
\textrm{AdS}_{d+1}
are given by the following parametrization,

\displaystyle X^{\mathsf{a}}=\frac{R}{z}x^{\mathsf{a}}\,,

\displaystyle X^{d}=\frac{R}{z}\frac{1-x^{2}-z^{2}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle X^{d+1}=\frac{R}{z}\frac{1+x^{2}+z^{2}}{2}\,,

(A.2)

such that for
R=\text{constant}
we have

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\textrm{AdS}_{d+1}}=g_{\mu\nu}dx^{\mu}dx^{\nu}=\frac{R^{2}}{z^{2}}\left(dz^{2}+dx_{\mathsf{a}}dx^{\mathsf{a}}\right)\,.

(A.3)

We can also view eq. (
A.2
) as a coordinate transformation from the Cartesian coorinates
X^{A}
to the coordinates
(R,z,x^{\mathsf{a}})
, under which metric of
\mathbb{R}^{d+1,1}
becomes

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{2,d}}=-dR^{2}+ds^{2}_{\textrm{AdS}_{d+1}}\,.

(A.4)

In fact,
(R,z,x^{\mathsf{a}})
are Gaussian normal coordinates adapted to the AdS hypersurface and the
g_{\mu\nu}
in eq. (
A.3
) is the induced metric.

When we study a scalar field
\phi
on
\textrm{AdS}_{d+1}
, it is convenient to extend its definition to the entire embedding space. However, we need to make sure that the dynamics does not depend on the variation normal to the AdS hypersurface. It is not difficult to show that

\displaystyle\eta^{AB}\partial_{A}\phi\partial_{B}\phi

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{R}\phi)^{2}+g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi\,,\qquad\frac{1}{R}X^{A}\partial_{A}\phi=\partial_{R}\phi\,.

(A.5)

We can thus define

\displaystyle G_{AB}=\eta_{AB}-\frac{X_{A}X_{B}}{X^{2}}=g^{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial X_{A}}{\partial x^{\mu}}\frac{\partial X_{B}}{\partial x^{\nu}}\,,

(A.6)

such that

\displaystyle G^{AB}\partial_{A}\phi\partial_{B}\phi=g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi\,.

(A.7)

One can also prove that the AdS Laplacian is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{A}(G^{AB}\partial_{B})=-\frac{1}{2}D_{X}^{2}=\nabla^{2}_{\textrm{AdS}}\,.

(A.8)

The matrix form of
G_{AB}
under the
(R,z,x^{\mathsf{a}})
coordinates is

\displaystyle G_{AB}\xrightarrow{\text{transformation}~\eqref{eq:embedding}}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&g_{\mu\nu}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(A.9)

namely,
G^{AB}
is indeed a projector to
\textrm{AdS}_{d+1}
. One can easily show that
G_{AB}
is idempotent and transverse to the normal vector
X^{A}
,

\displaystyle G_{A}{}^{B}G_{B}{}^{C}=G_{A}{}^{C}\,,\qquad G_{AB}X^{B}=0\,.

(A.10)

Geometrically,
G_{AB}
is the first fundamental form associated to the hypersurface (
A.1
). From now on, we fix
R=1
for the AdS hypersurface, such that the AdS integration measure becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\textrm{AdS}}dX\equiv\int_{\textrm{AdS}}\frac{\delta(R-1)R^{d+1}dRdzd^{d}x}{z^{d+1}}=\int_{\textrm{AdS}}\frac{dzdx^{d}}{z^{d+1}}\,.

(A.11)

Thus we can perform integration-by-parts for
\partial/\partial X^{A}
if it appears in the integrand as
\frac{\partial}{\partial X^{A}}(G^{AB}\cdots)
or
G^{BA}\frac{\partial}{\partial X^{A}}(\cdots)
, since the projector
G^{AB}
removes
\frac{\partial}{\partial R}
so the measure remains invariant.

A tensor
H_{A_{1}A_{2}\ldots A_{n}}
in the embedding space defines a tensor on AdS if it is
transverse
to the AdS hypersurface,

\displaystyle X^{A_{i}}H_{A_{1}A_{2}\ldots A_{n}}=0\,,\qquad 1\leqslant i\leqslant n\,.

(A.12)

The AdS tensor can be recovered through the projection

\displaystyle h_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\ldots\mu_{n}}=\frac{\partial X^{A_{1}}}{\partial x^{\mu_{1}}}\frac{\partial X^{A_{2}}}{\partial x^{\mu_{2}}}\ldots\frac{\partial X^{A_{n}}}{\partial x^{\mu_{n}}}H_{A_{1}A_{2}\ldots A_{n}}\,.

(A.13)

From this equation and the relation (
A.6
) between
g_{\mu\nu}
and
G_{AB}
, we can show that the contraction between two AdS tensors can be uplifted into the embedding space,

\displaystyle h_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\ldots\mu_{n}}g^{\mu_{i}\nu_{j}}f_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\ldots\nu_{m}}=H_{A_{1}A_{2}\ldots A_{n}}G^{A_{i}B_{j}}F_{B_{1}B_{2}\ldots B_{m}}=H_{A_{1}A_{2}\ldots A_{n}}\eta^{A_{i}B_{j}}F_{B_{1}B_{2}\ldots B_{m}}\,,

(A.14)

where the second equality is due to the transversality. Conditions like being symmetric and traceless of an AdS tensor can be directly imposed on its embedding space uplift. A more formal discussion on this topic can be found in ref.
Costa:2014kfa
.

The conformal boundary of AdS is located at
z\rightarrow 0
. We can represent a boundary point
x_{i}^{\mathsf{a}}
by a projective null vector in the embedding space,

\displaystyle P_{i}=\Big(x_{i}^{\mathsf{a}},\frac{1-x_{i}^{2}}{2},\frac{1+x_{i}^{2}}{2}\Big)\,.

(A.15)

Obviously we have
P_{i}^{2}=0
and we identify
P_{i}\sim\lambda P_{k}
. A polarization vector
\epsilon_{i}^{\mathsf{a}}
on the boundary can also be represented in the embedding space,

\displaystyle Z_{i}=(\epsilon_{i}^{\mathsf{a}},-\epsilon_{i}\cdot x_{i},\epsilon_{i}\cdot x_{i})\,,

(A.16)

such that
P_{i}\cdot Z_{i}=0
and
Z_{i}\cdot Z_{i}=\epsilon_{i}^{2}=0
. A tensor current
F_{A_{1}A_{2}\ldots A_{n}}(P)
defined on the null cone
P^{2}=0
is physical only if it is homogeneous and transverse,

\displaystyle F_{A_{1}A_{2}\ldots A_{n}}(\lambda P)=\lambda^{-\Delta}F_{A_{1}A_{2}\ldots A_{n}}(P)\,,\qquad P^{A_{i}}F_{A_{1}A_{2}\ldots A_{n}}(P)=0\,,\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant n\,.

(A.17)

Finally, we list a few useful relations that connect the embedding space and physical space expressions,

\displaystyle-2X\cdot P_{i}

\displaystyle=z+z^{-1}(x-x_{i})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle-2P_{i}\cdot P_{j}

\displaystyle=(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle P_{i}\cdot Z_{j}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{j}\cdot(x_{i}-x_{j})\,,

\displaystyle Z_{i}\cdot Z_{j}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{j}\,,

(A.18)

where
X
is a bulk point defined as in eq. (
A.2
).

Appendix B
Contact diagrams in AdS

Contact diagrams of scalar operators play a crucial role in flat space scattering amplitudes. Crucially, the delta function that imposes momentum conservation on an
n
-point scattering amplitudes arises from the contact diagram of an
n
-point scalar vertex

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{\textrm{contact}}^{\textrm{flat}}&=\int d^{d}x\prod_{i}e^{ip_{i}x}\\
&=\delta^{d}\Big(\sum_{i}p_{i}^{\mu}\Big)\end{split}

(B.1)

The contact diagrams of
n
-point scalar vertex operators play an analogous role in AdS, giving the AdS analog of the flat space delta function. The contact diagram associated with the vertex
\prod_{i}\phi_{i}
in AdS is

\mathcal{A}^{n}_{\textrm{contact}}=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}{2\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(\Delta_{i}-d/2+1)}\right)D_{\Delta_{1}\dots\Delta_{n}}

(B.2)

where

\displaystyle D_{\Delta_{1}\dots\Delta_{n}}=\int_{\textrm{AdS}}dX\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(-2P_{i}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{i}}}

(B.3)

is the
D
-function. Any tree-level position space correlator in AdS will be a polynomial in
D
-functions,
Z
-variables and
P
-variables. Similar to how all flat space scattering amplitudes contain a universal momentum conserving delta function, each term in the polynomial expansion of the position space correlator contains its own
D
-function. A major challenge for evaluating position space correlators in AdS is finding representations of
D
-functions amiable to numeric approximation.

B.1
Three-point
D
-functions

The
D
-functions that appear in three-point correlators can be integrated directly:

\displaystyle D_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\textrm{AdS}}dX\prod_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{(-2P_{i}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{i}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{2\prod_{i}\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\Gamma\left(\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}-d}{2}\right)\prod_{i<j}\frac{\Gamma(\delta_{ij})}{(-2P_{i}\cdot P_{j})^{\delta_{ij}}}

(B.4)

\displaystyle\delta_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{i}+\Delta_{j}-\Delta_{k})\ .

The three-point
D
-function is the one which has a rational dependence on coordinates; higher point
D
-functions are rather non-trivial functions.

B.2
Exact solution of four-point
D
-functions

In this section, we review a derivation of an exact solution to the four-point
D
-function in terms of derivatives of polylogarithms. Advantages (and disadvantages) of alternative methods are discussed at the end.

To find a numerically tractable representation of eq. (
B.3
), we first rewrote the
D
-function into a Feynman parameterization:

D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}=\frac{\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(\frac{\Sigma-d}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\Sigma}{2})}{2\prod_{i}\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\int\frac{\prod_{j}\alpha_{j}^{\Delta_{j}-1}\delta(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}-1)}{(\sum_{k,l}\alpha_{k}\alpha_{l}P_{k,l})^{\Sigma/2}}\,,

(B.5)

where
\Sigma=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}
. Our goal is to find an exact solution for the integral in eq. (
B.5
) that can be used for numerical analysis in Mathematica. We first solve for the integral in eq. (
B.5
) for the simplest case when
\Delta_{i}=1
for all
i
’s, which we denote as
B(P_{ij})
. It is nothing but the four-mass box integral
Denner:1991qq
;
Bern:1993kr
,

\begin{split}B(P_{ij})&=\int\frac{\prod_{j}d\alpha_{j}\delta(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}-1)}{\left(\sum_{k,l}\alpha_{k}\alpha_{l}P_{k,l}\right)}\\
&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta^{\prime}}}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{u_{+}u_{-}}{(1-u_{+})^{2}(1-u_{-})^{2}}\right)\log\frac{u_{+}}{u_{-}}\\
&\quad-\textrm{Li}_{2}(1-u_{+})+\textrm{Li}_{2}(1-u_{-})-\textrm{Li}_{2}\Big(1-\frac{1}{u_{-}}\Big)+\textrm{Li}_{2}\Big(1-\frac{1}{u_{+}}\Big)\Bigg],\end{split}

(B.6)

where

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}=X^{2}+Y^{2}+Z^{2}-2XY-2YZ-2ZX\,,

\displaystyle X=P_{12}P_{34}\,,

\displaystyle Y=P_{13}P_{24}\,,

\displaystyle u_{\pm}=\frac{Y+X-Z\pm\sqrt{\Delta^{\prime}}}{2Y}\,,

\displaystyle Z=P_{14}P_{23}\,.

(B.7)

Note that eq. (
B.6
) is simply the standard four-mass box integral whose solution has been known for 20 odd years.
17
17

17

The same integral was evaluated in eq. (39) in ref.
Bianchi:1998nk
, but their solution appears to have a typo. One can test the relative sign of individual terms in a given convention by checking whether the resulting
D
-function obeys the properties given in appendix
B.3
.
We then identify a simply relation between derivatives of
B(P_{ij})
and
D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}
to find a generic solution for four-point
D
-functions:

\begin{split}&\quad\frac{\pi^{d/2}(-1)^{\sum_{i<j}c_{ij}}\Gamma(\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}-d}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}}{2})}{2\Gamma(2+\sum_{i<j}c_{ij})(\prod_{i}\Gamma(\Delta_{i}^{\prime}))}\left[\prod_{i<j}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial P_{ij}}\right)^{c_{ij}}\right]B(P_{ij})\\
&=\frac{\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}-d}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\Sigma^{\prime}}{2})}{2(\prod_{i}\Gamma(\Delta_{i}^{\prime}))}\int\frac{\prod_{a}d\alpha_{a}\alpha_{a}^{\Delta^{\prime}_{a}-1}\delta(\sum_{b}\alpha_{b}-1)}{\left(\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}P_{ij}\right)^{\Sigma^{\prime}/2}}\\
&=D_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\Delta_{2}^{\prime},\Delta_{3}^{\prime},\Delta_{4}^{\prime}}\,,\end{split}

(B.8)

where
\Delta_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\neq i}c_{ij}+1
and
\Sigma^{\prime}=\sum_{i}\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
. Equations (
B.6
) and (
B.8
) together provide a closed form expressions for arbitrary four-point
D
-functions in terms of derivatives of polylogarithms. Unfortunately, although this representation is advantageous in that it provides an
exact
representation of the
D
-function in terms of polylogarithms, the
D
-function expressions become cumbersome very quickly, even for symbolic computation programs such as Mathematica. We ultimately used numeric differentiation algorithms in combination with eqs. (
B.6
) and (
B.8
) to solve for the
D
-functions at arbitrary kinematic points. These results were cross-checked with more direct numeric integrations of eq. (
B.5
).

Finding numerically tractable representations of
D
-functions is generically quite hard. For example, although standard Mellin integral representations of the
D
-function can be integrated numerically with high precision, defining the actual contour for integration is somewhat subtle. While the Mellin representation of Feynman integrals is largely understood, the problem seems to be slightly more technically challenging for the
D
-functions corresponding to AdS contact diagrams. One might hope that numeric integration in the Feynman representation would offer a more realistic approach, as it suffers from no contour ambiguities. Unfortunately, direct numeric integration of Feynman parameterized integrals is often unstable for arbitrary kinematics and
\Delta_{i}
unless sophisticated weighted Monte-Carlo sampling techniques are applied. For example, see ref.
Borinsky:2020rqs
.

B.3
D
-function identities

When computing NLSM and YM AdS boundary correlators, we have used some
D
-function identities to simplify the results. In this appendix, we derive these identities starting from the conformal Ward identity. We start with the four-point case,

\displaystyle(D_{1}^{AB}+D_{2}^{AB}+D_{3}^{AB}+D_{4}^{AB})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}=0\,.

(B.9)

We can act respectively
(D_{1}+D_{3})^{AB}
and
(D_{2}+D_{4})^{AB}
onto this equation, giving

\displaystyle(2D_{13}^{2}+D_{1}^{2}+D_{3}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}=-(D_{1}+D_{3})_{AB}(D_{2}+D_{4})^{AB}D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}\,,

\displaystyle(2D_{24}^{2}+D_{2}^{2}+D_{4}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}=-(D_{2}+D_{4})_{AB}(D_{1}+D_{3})^{AB}D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}\,.

(B.10)

Since
(D_{1}+D_{3})
commutes with
(D_{2}+D_{4})
and
D_{i}^{2}=-2m_{i}^{2}=-2\Delta_{i}(\Delta_{i}-d)
when acting on a conformal partial wave, we get

\displaystyle(D_{23}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}=(D_{14}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}\,.

(B.11)

In particular, when
\Delta_{i}=\Delta
, we have

\displaystyle D_{13}^{2}D_{\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta}=D_{24}^{2}D_{\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta}\,.

(B.12)

The above relation can be easily generalized to
n
-points,

\displaystyle\Big(D_{I}^{2}-\sum_{i\in I}m_{i}^{2}\Big)D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\ldots,\Delta_{n}}=\Big(D_{\bar{I}}^{2}-\sum_{i\in\bar{I}}m_{i}^{2}\Big)D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\ldots,\Delta_{n}}\,,

(B.13)

where
I\cup\bar{I}=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
and
I\cap\bar{I}=\emptyset
.

We can also carry out the derivatives in eq. (
B.11
) explicitly to obtain relations involving boundary positions. For example, we can use

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}D_{13}^{2}D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}=4\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}(P_{1}\cdot P_{3})D_{\Delta_{1}+1,\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}+1,\Delta_{4}}+\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}\,,

(B.14)

and a similar equation for
D_{24}^{2}
to show that

\displaystyle(P_{1}\cdot P_{3})D_{\Delta+1,\Delta,\Delta+1,\Delta}=(P_{2}\cdot P_{4})D_{\Delta,\Delta+1,\Delta,\Delta+1}\quad\text{ for }\Delta_{i}=\Delta\,.

(B.15)

One can also show that acting
D_{12}^{2}
and
D_{13}^{2}
consecutively gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}D_{12}^{2}D_{13}^{2}D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=16\Delta_{1}(\Delta_{1}+1)\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}(P_{1}\!\cdot\!P_{2})(P_{1}\!\cdot\!P_{3})D_{\Delta_{1}+2,\Delta_{2}+1,\Delta_{3}+1,\Delta_{4}}

(B.16)

\displaystyle\quad+4\Delta_{1}(\Delta_{1}+1)\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Big[(P_{1}\!\cdot\!P_{2})D_{\Delta_{1}+1,\Delta_{2}+1,\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}+(2\leftrightarrow 3)\Big]

\displaystyle\quad-4\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}(P_{2}\!\cdot\!P_{3})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}+1,\Delta_{3}+1,\Delta_{4}}+\Delta_{1}^{2}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}\,,

which is symmetric under the
2\leftrightarrow 3
exchange. This also means that
D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}
lives in the kernel of
[D_{12}^{2},D_{13}^{2}]
, namely,
[D_{12}^{2},D_{13}^{2}]D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}=0
.

Next, we act
D_{1}^{AB}
onto eq. (
B.9
) and then use eq. (
B.11
) to eliminate
D_{14}^{2}
. This gives

\displaystyle(D_{12}^{2}+D_{13}^{2}+D_{23}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}+(m_{4}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}=0\,.

(B.17)

When
\Delta_{i}=\Delta
, we get

\displaystyle(D_{12}^{2}+D_{13}^{2}+D_{23}^{2})D_{\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta}=2\Delta(\Delta-d)D_{\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta}\,.

(B.18)

This equation also implies the identity

\displaystyle(P_{1}\cdot P_{2})D_{\Delta+1,\Delta+1,\Delta,\Delta}

\displaystyle+(P_{1}\cdot P_{3})D_{\Delta+1,\Delta,\Delta+1,\Delta}

\displaystyle+(P_{2}\cdot P_{3})D_{\Delta,\Delta+1,\Delta+1,\Delta}=-\frac{4\Delta-d}{4\Delta}D_{\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta}\,.

(B.19)

In the on-shell limit
\Delta=d
, we thus derive the relation

\displaystyle(D_{12}^{2}+D_{13}^{2}+D_{23}^{2})D_{d,d,d,d,}=0\,,

(B.20)

which resembles the relation
s+t+u=0
for flat space Mandelstam variables.

The above identities can also be derived using integration-by-parts relations. Below we show the derivation of eq. (
B.11
). Our calculation will be given in the embedding space, which is in parallel with the one in the physical space
DHoker:1999kzh
. To start with, we define

\displaystyle D_{\partial\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\partial\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{\textrm{AdS}}dXG^{AB}\Bigg[\frac{\partial}{\partial X^{A}}\frac{1}{(-2P_{1}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{1}}}\Bigg]\frac{1}{(-2P_{2}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\quad\!\times\Bigg[\frac{\partial}{\partial X^{B}}\frac{1}{(-2P_{3}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{3}}}\Bigg]\frac{1}{(-2P_{4}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{4}}}

\displaystyle=4\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}(P_{1}\cdot P_{3})D_{\Delta_{1}+1,\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}+1,\Delta_{4}}+\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}\,.

(B.21)

We then use the identity

\displaystyle\partial_{A}G^{AB}\partial_{B}\left[\frac{1}{(-2P_{1}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{1}}}\frac{1}{(-2P_{3}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{3}}}\right]

\displaystyle=2G^{AB}\frac{\partial}{\partial X^{A}}\frac{1}{(-2P_{1}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{1}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial X^{B}}\frac{1}{(-2P_{3}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{3}}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{m_{1}^{2}+m_{3}^{2}}{(-2P_{1}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{1}}(-2P_{3}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{3}}}

(B.22)

to write eq. (
B.3
) as

\displaystyle D_{\partial\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\partial\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}+\frac{1}{2}(m_{1}^{2}+m_{3}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\textrm{AdS}}dX(\partial_{A}G^{AB}\partial_{B})\left[\frac{1}{(-2P_{1}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{1}}}\frac{1}{(-2P_{3}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{3}}}\right]\frac{1}{(-2P_{2}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{2}}}\frac{1}{(-2P_{4}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{4}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\textrm{AdS}}dX(\partial_{A}G^{AB}\partial_{B})\left[\frac{1}{(-2P_{2}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{2}}}\frac{1}{(-2P_{4}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{4}}}\right]\frac{1}{(-2P_{1}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{1}}}\frac{1}{(-2P_{3}\cdot X)^{\Delta_{3}}}

\displaystyle=D_{\Delta_{1},\partial\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\partial\Delta_{4}}+\frac{1}{2}(m_{2}^{2}+m_{4}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}\,,

(B.23)

where integration-by-parts has been used to obtain the second equation. This leads to the identity

\displaystyle\quad 4\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}(P_{1}\cdot P_{3})D_{\Delta_{1}+1,\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}+1,\Delta_{4}}+\frac{1}{2}(2\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}+m_{1}^{2}+m_{3}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=4\Delta_{2}\Delta_{4}(P_{2}\cdot P_{4})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}+1,\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}+1}+\frac{1}{2}(2\Delta_{2}\Delta_{4}+m_{2}^{2}+m_{4}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}\,.

(B.24)

When
\Delta_{i}=\Delta
, we can recover
(P_{1}\cdot P_{3})D_{\Delta+1,\Delta,\Delta+1,\Delta}=(P_{2}\cdot P_{4})D_{\Delta,\Delta+1,\Delta,\Delta+1}
. Now using eq. (
B.14
) to replace
P_{i}\cdot P_{j}
by
D_{13}^{2}
immediately leads to

\displaystyle(D_{23}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}=(D_{14}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})D_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}\,.

(B.25)

Appendix C
Derivation of eq. (
6.36
)

In this appendix, we give a derivation of eq. (
6.36
), copied here as

\displaystyle\textbf{M}^{-1}\Bigg[\prod_{1\leqslant i<j}^{4}\frac{\Gamma(\delta_{ij}+l_{ij})}{\Gamma(\delta_{ij})}\Bigg]=\frac{2}{\pi^{d/2}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma(\tilde{\Delta}_{i})}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{\tilde{\Sigma}-d}{2}\Big)}\Bigg[\prod_{1\leqslant i<j}^{4}P_{ij}^{l_{ij}}\Bigg]D_{\tilde{\Delta}_{1}\tilde{\Delta}_{2}\tilde{\Delta}_{3}\tilde{\Delta}_{4}}\ .

(C.1)

It suffices to consider the inverse Mellin transform of

\frac{\Gamma(\delta_{12}+l)}{\Gamma(\delta_{12})}\ .

(C.2)

We first parameterize the delta functions in the inverse Mellin transform (
6.37
)

\delta(\Delta_{i}-\sum_{j}\delta_{ij})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt_{i}}{t_{i}}t_{i}^{\Delta_{i}-\sum_{j}\delta_{ij}}

(C.3)

and then evaluate the
\delta_{ij}
contour integral using

\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\delta_{ij}}{2\pi i}\Gamma(\delta_{ij}+l)(t_{i}t_{j}P_{ij})^{-\delta_{ij}}=(t_{i}t_{j}P_{ij})^{l}e^{-t_{i}t_{j}P_{ij}}\ .

(C.4)

This leads to

\textbf{M}^{-1}\Bigg[\frac{\Gamma(\delta_{12}+l)}{\Gamma(\delta_{12})}\Bigg]=(P_{12})^{l}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{4}\frac{dt_{i}}{t_{i}}\Bigg]t_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+l}t_{2}^{\Delta_{2}+l}t_{3}^{\Delta_{3}}t_{4}^{\Delta_{4}}\prod_{i<j}e^{-t_{i}t_{j}P_{ij}}\ .

(C.5)

To convert this expression to a
D
-function, we first insert the identity element

\begin{split}1&=\frac{1}{\Gamma[(\Delta_{1}+l)/2+(\Delta_{2}+l)/2+\Delta_{3}/2+\Delta_{4}/2-d/2]}\\
&\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dz}{z}z^{-d/2+(\Delta_{1}+l)/2+(\Delta_{2}+l)/2+\Delta_{3}/2+\Delta_{4}/2}e^{-z}\end{split}

(C.6)

and then rescale
t_{i}\rightarrow t_{i}^{\prime}/\sqrt{z}
. Now we can carry out the
z
integral as

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dz}{z}z^{-d/2}\exp\Big[-z+\frac{1}{z}\Big(\sum_{i}t^{\prime}_{i}P_{i}\Big)^{2}\Big]=\int_{\textrm{AdS}}dX\prod_{i}e^{2t_{i}^{\prime}X\cdot P_{i}}\,.

(C.7)

Finally, we perform the
t^{\prime}_{i}
integral as

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt^{\prime}_{i}}{t^{\prime}_{i}}(t^{\prime}_{i})^{\Delta^{\prime}_{i}}e^{t^{\prime}(2X\cdot P_{i})}=\frac{\Gamma(\Delta^{\prime}_{i})}{(-2X\cdot P_{i})^{\Delta^{\prime}_{i}}}

(C.8)

to reach our desired result

\textbf{M}^{-1}\Bigg[\frac{\Gamma(\delta_{12}+l)}{\Gamma(\delta_{12})}\Bigg]=\frac{2}{\pi^{d/2}}P_{1,2}^{l}\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_{1}+l)\Gamma(\Delta_{2}+l)\Gamma(\Delta_{3})\Gamma(\Delta_{4})D_{\Delta_{1}+l_{1},\Delta_{2}+l_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}}}{\Gamma[(\Delta_{1}+l)/2+(\Delta_{2}+l)/2+\Delta_{3}/2+\Delta_{4}/2-d/2]}\ .

(C.9)

It is trivial to see that eq. (
C.9
) generalizes to eq. (
6.36
).
